It's a total sum of 6 billion schilling. The German Foundation is addressed to all German companies in the trade register with more than ten employees. The idea behind this is: The entire German economy depends on each other. The German economy has only entered into commitments of about 3 billion D-Mark, because in terms of legal certainty there is no contract between Germany and the USA. Only in an exchange of letters between Clinton and Schröder does President Clinton ensure with so-called "statements of interest" legal certainty that the US government wants to assist German companies in the case of complaints. Ladies and gentlemen! In Austria, one has for the time being approached the large companies with more than 250 employees. The aim is to obtain 0.2 percent of the previous year's revenue for the fund. More should be done by the construction industry, e-economics and railways – keyword: Impact factor. The conditions for the grants are to be agreed with the USA on a so-called legal basis. Let's look over the border towards the USA again: In 1988 the United States set a gesture of reconciliation. After the Japanese attack on the US fleet base Pearl Harbor at the end of 1941, 120 000 US citizens of Japanese descent had been taken to detention camps until the end of World War II. The United States was afraid that they could not behave loyally to their new home country. For those still living in 1988, some 60 000 former prisoners were decided under President Reagan each $20 000 compensation payments. According to "Neue Zürcher Zeitung" of 20. In June of this year, former U.S. prisoners of war who had been employed in large Japanese companies during the Second World War have filed compensation cases against the US subsidiaries of these large Japanese companies. It was possible to abolish the actions by means of a new California law which abolished the limitation of claims for compensation. You hear and marvel: However, the US companies strongly reject the claims for compensation. The reason is, among other things, that they are not legal successors to the Japanese companies that employed forced workers during the war. I personally consider the reconciliation fund law to be important, right and good from its intention and development. Ladies and gentlemen! It is clear to me that grants of every kind from Austria must be linked to a condition, namely to the assurance of permanent legal certainty and lasting legal peace on the part of the United States. – Therefore: No collective actions, no rolling up of legally completed compensation procedures and limitation of the claims in the room from the so-called arration question – keyword: 70 000 rental apartments, keyword: operating solutions. The Reconciliation Fund Act is a voluntary, generous gesture, and the Reconciliation Fund Act must also be understood. Austria of the year 2000 is a sovereign state because of the Vienna State Treaty 1955 – we have already heard it – which legally owes no longer any repair payments. The good will of the Federal Government, which manifests itself in the Reconciliation Fund Act, is obvious. For me, all steps of reconciliation are to be understood as the end of a chapter of the history of the last century, in which we all did not participate actively. – By the way: As a result, I hope that those countries that have used Austrians as forced workers will also set reconciliation stones. It has our country, it has done a lot in the past 55 years in order to reach the hand and set gestures of reconciliation. This gesture setting cannot be continued ad infinitum. For me as a mother, it is important not to pass on to our children and children any financial burden from the past century. Tomorrow generations will be able to reach the previous generations in dignity.

It has been charged in over 28 000 cases, 14 000 charges have been made with punishments such as the withdrawal of freedom, the ban on the profession and the loss of the office, 43 people have been sentenced to death, 30 judgments have been executed, and some 100 000 officials have been suspended from the public service. However, it must not be overlooked, ladies and gentlemen, that the importance of this novelle will be limited, because, according to estimates, it is in practice a question of delivering around 100 to 200 CD-ROMs to the Austrian National Library in the year. The question still remains as to how the legislator imagines the flood of information and the collection of information and offers of the Internet. We must not forget that we are now dealing with a two-class society of publishers of information: on the one hand, those who publish printing works, then the so-called offline products subject to the obligation to offer and deliver, and on the other hand, the producers of online services on the Internet that do not have to offer and deliver. To this end, a working group has been set up to check whether it is technically feasible that such comprehensive archiving can then take place on the Internet for the benefit of the Austrian National Library. In this enormous speed of electronic investigation, it is not unlikely that this will be the case, but if it is not possible – whether it is not possible for the State community to adopt general standards in this respect and not to extend this obligation to offer and deliver products to online products here in Austria – I believe that, in my opinion, the obligation to deliver and offer is to be questioned at all, because a two-class society cannot be maintained in the long term.

It is simply an important symbol and also means concrete action. Mrs Stoisits has rightly pointed out what is actually a state objective. The Austrian Constitution is very reticent with such programmatic statements. This is related to the philosophy behind it. ==References== In November 1920, almost 80 years ago, the Constitution entered into force and contains very few such provisions. On the one hand, it only reflected a minimum consensus of all political parties. In addition, this is also characteristic of the formal understanding of the constitutional creators of 1920. More recently, this is different. Since the mid-1970s, we have included in the Constitution a number of interesting points of programme declarations. One is the commitment to comprehensive national defence, party democracy, the declaration of the public service role of Austrian broadcasting and television, and in recent times the equal treatment of disabled people and equality between men and women. It can be seen that very important issues have already been included in the Constitution in the programme. The treatment and protection of the ethnic groups and their rights is another very important point. The speakers are, of course, right: It is not directly applicable to the individual. That's true. But for the administration as a whole, the national objective must be applied directly. It is, on the one hand, the scale for interpretations and, on the other, a range of considerations for discretion. For example, the State objective also serves as a measure of interpretation for jurisdiction, especially in the area of jurisdiction of public law. To date, the Constitutional Court has assumed in its case law that the relevant constitutional standards contain a value decision in favour of minority protection. This is now explained and explained in more detail and, depending on the subject matter of law, there can be a direct preference for the protection of members of a minority vis-à-vis the members of other social groups. This may sound formal, but is actually very easy to interpret. The State objective includes the legislative and enforcement mandate to live up to the State objective through concrete measures. We have not only done rhetoric, I would like to stress that. We have just announced a topography regulation for the Burgenland in the first weeks of this federal government. The application of German-Croatian and German-Hungarian designations is planned in several municipalities of the Burgenland. ==References== In June the Federal Government appointed the Hungarian official language regulation. We have now taken some substantial steps in the field of minority school law. So I would say that, in the last few weeks and months, what is rightly in the working programme or in the expectant programme of the ethnic groups has been done quite fruitfully, and we want to continue. We will now start working on a modern People's Group Act, and you can be sure that you will of course also be involved in these discussions. I would therefore ask that this really be taken as an important step today, which is perhaps also a little determined by the current report situation, for example by the fact that the Council of Wise Men will look at what is happening in Austria in the direction of minorities, asylum and refugees. I have said that I will also take out the freedom to appoint an international professor or scientist myself to get a comparative overview of what other European countries are doing. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and the Freeman.]] I'll tell you: Austria does not in any way need to hide the result because of what has been of course a legal existence in the past. But perhaps there will be some positive competition, which I believe could only benefit Europe and its minorities. Therefore: We are not afraid either, we are not defensive, and if the debate helps to improve things across Europe, then Austria has once again pointed the way.

This acceptance is highly precarious. A rational discussion of the legal problems of ethnic minorities is overlaid by emotions, prejudices and fears. The prejudices and fears of the stranger – in a broad sense of this word – have grown alarmingly in Austria in recent years. It is especially the immigration of foreigners – that is, foreigners in a narrow legal sense – and the associated emergence of new ethnic groups that have triggered or strengthened such fears in many Austrians. The ‘autochthonian’ ethnic groups in Austria are in so far only indirectly victims of this development. Those who feel the diversity of ethnic groups in their own country as a wealth – and we do – "will have such developments against problems of understanding. However, those who ignore the prejudices and fears of strangers in many people and want to enforce their ideals without regard to them risk serious disappointments. The right is a very inadequate instrument to counteract such emotional phenomena. It can only support the necessary consciousness on point, but depends to a great extent on the state of the social consciousness. People's policy should therefore be, above all, a policy of information and information. Law cannot replace a lack of information and information policy. However, it cannot be excluded – and this is not because the situation of ethnic groups is determined by their rights. The necessary information and information work must be aimed at preparing the necessary acceptance of such rights to the ground. Regulation must not, on the other hand, ignore the horizon of social consciousness." - That's Öhlinger. I therefore come to this motion for a resolution on the death penalty. It must not be forgotten that the present materials were unanimously decided that we would not close ourselves to a constructive law and a constructive proposal and that we would not conclude a constructive dialogue if we believed that this was a useful thing. In our view, the already negotiated Reconciliation Fund Act is a positive thing, it is the objective of the state, and it is also this request concerning the death penalty, which is probably also to be decided unanimously, a positive and therefore a consensual and therefore unanimous matter. Although we will give our agreement, we will nevertheless allow ourselves to say that which is a matter, because we do not want to be killed by the policy we have, by the policy we deeply reject and from which we are highly dissociating. The present proposal, this unanimous resolution, is a call to the Federal Government. The main points are four: to ensure that the death penalty is abolished worldwide, in particular in the Council of Europe, to press for the ratification of an agreement in place there, to force the debate on the death penalty, particularly in talks with the candidate countries on the European Union; to discuss this also in the UN General Assembly, and in particular to protect children and young people from the death penalty. That was our concern. In Austria, there has always been a unanimous rejection of the death penalty. In Austria, the death penalty was abolished in 1968, and there is a broad social consensus that the death penalty is not an appropriate instrument of execution. The evidence of this is also evidence of the practice. In Austria, we have a low rate of crime, especially in crimes against body and life, which also proves that the death penalty does not have a dissuasive character but has a stimulating character and that there is a close link between the excessive imposition of death penalty and violence. The 1999 report of "amnesty international" shows that a total of 1,625 executions have still been carried out in 37 states, that the death penalty is still legislated in 90 states and that it has been or will be excessively enforced in many countries. The leader is the People's Republic of China with 1 067 executions, followed by Zaire with 100, then already follow the United States of America with 68 and Iran with 66 executions; the extra-legal executions, the disappearance and so on are not taken into account. The death penalty violates the supreme human right, namely the right to life. It also violates the integrity of people in that they are kept in tiny death cells, which is equal to a torture. And that people are deliberately killed in front of spectators in particular underlines the speculative nature of the executions. How executions are carried out, you take a picture of it yourself; for example, I can remind you of the speculative media coverage, of how cruel and cruel this reporting is already. In addition, the death penalty is often imposed on racial or social aspects. On average, members of minorities are executed. In the United States, for example, 35 percent of prisoners are black in death contracts, although the proportion of blacks in the total population is only 12 percent. And unfortunately, the execution of mental illness and youth is also on the agenda. Therefore, to be opposed to the death penalty does not mean to forget the victims of violence and their families or to show no compassion, nor to appreciate the suffering of the survivors accordingly, but it is simply a barbaric act of execution. One has to address the causes of the crimes, the death penalty itself is just a link in the chain of violence. A death penalty statistics, briefly quoted, shows that, for example, in the US in those States where the death penalty is imposed, the crime rate in relation to murder is much higher than in States where there is no death penalty, which is significantly higher. Statistics show that, for example, in the south of the United States, where 80 percent of the executions have been carried out, the murder rate is the highest, the north east with only 1 percent of the executions having the lowest murder rate by far. The comparison of eight industrialized states also shows that in the United States, the only one of these eight industrialized states that impose the death penalty, far more people die from firearms than in the rest. For example – to make it clear to you –: In the U.S., 35 people are killed by firearms, in Switzerland 13.6, in Sweden 4, in Canada 3, in Germany 2.5, in Australia 0.3, in New Zealand 0.6, in the UK 0.5. And it also shows that despite an excessive increase in executions in the US, the murder rate remains relatively stable. I believe that I have demonstrated that the death penalty is not an appropriate means of enforcement, and this motion for a resolution that is manifestly in motion may therefore be a small contribution to solving the problem. The government should strongly support initiatives, especially since Austria, especially in the Council of Europe, has always been a pioneer in abolishing the death penalty and is still there.

ParlaMint-AT_2000-07-07-021-XXI-NRSITZ-00034_d7e8404	Mr President. Mr State Secretary! My venerations Ladies and gentlemen! 1 813 people were executed in 31 countries alone in 1999, according to official figures. 3 857 people in 63 countries were sentenced to death. In Iran, Saudi Arabia and the USA, the number of executions in 1999 has even increased, although the total number of executions has decreased. In view of these figures, it is the duty of those countries - Mr Posch has already done this correctly - in which the death penalty is prohibited from voting against it. The right of man to his life is at the centre of the fundamental protection system. The fundamental right protects against arbitrary intervention into human life and its self-protection. In Austria, the death penalty was considered compatible for a long time. It was only in 1968, that is, in the time of an ÖVP Chancellor, that it was banned without exception by Article 85 of the Federal Constitutional Act. I would like to stress that every person's right of life exists from the beginning of his physical-spiritual existence to death. The right to life therefore also comes to the embryo. It is our duty to protect the unborn life too! It is surprising and, in my opinion, deeply embarrassing that such states as the USA, which are often so vehemently committed to the protection of human rights, still have the right to life with the imposition and execution of death penalty. In 1999, 98 prisoners were executed in the United States alone. Often the death penalty in the US and other countries is justified by the need to prevent and protect society. These two reasons are largely disputed, because they do not justify the death penalty, nor do they achieve the desired effect with the death penalty, as investigations clearly prove. In addition, a conviction always poses the danger of error. There is therefore a danger that innocent people will be executed. Between 1973 and mid-1999, 87 prisoners were acquitted from the death penalty in the United States. The death penalty itself is cruel and despicable, but the notion that innocent people are killed is disgusting. [[General condolences.]] Ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen! Human rights are indivisible. Revenge of relatives of victims and the task of the state to ensure the public order and the security of people do not justify the death penalty. Today's society, it should be thought, must have grown beyond such cruel, humane methods and act with more far-sightedness, but above all respect for human life. In this context, I would like to stress the achievements of NGOs who do valuable work to raise awareness and to disseminate knowledge on the subject of death penalty and are tirelessly committed to their abolition. I therefore see it as our duty to point out and address these abuses. For this reason, the Federal Government, in particular the Foreign Minister, is asked to step up its efforts on every opportunity to address the global abolition of the death penalty. Point 2 of the request calls for the ratification of the corresponding agreement by the Member States within the Council of Europe. This point seems to me to be particularly important. In 1997, 17 executions were carried out in the Council of Europe. At the end of 1997, these executions were strongly condemned by the Council of Europe and were faced with the threat of rethinking the membership of the countries concerned in the course of this practice. In Article 2, the European Convention on Human Rights recognises the human right of life and obliges the State to protect this right legally. In addition, the Additional Protocol No 6 to the European Convention on Human Rights specifically prohibits the condemnation of the death penalty and its execution. Since 1994, nine Member States of the Council of Europe have been required to abolish the death penalty within a period of three years. Unfortunately, not all Member States have ratified the Additional Protocol; Turkey has not yet done so, Ukraine, Georgia, Poland and Albania. Although in the past the ban on the death penalty was not a condition for the Council of Europe to be accepted, this should in future be a prerequisite for this and for all existing members a desired goal. Compliance with these provisions should be strictly controlled and infringements should be severely sanctioned. In particular, the members of the European Union should work together to abolish the death penalty worldwide, for Europe is a community of values and should be particularly united in the defence and protection of human rights and communicate and document it accordingly abroad.

ParlaMint-AT_2000-07-07-021-XXI-NRSITZ-00034_d7e10775	Mr President. Mrs. Federal Minister! High house! Dear ladies and gentlemen! In the revision of the Study Promotion Act 1992 it is clear in the explanations at the beginning that our current Studienförderungsgesetz is purely designed for full-time studies. Considering, at the same time, that only about 42 percent of all students are full-time students, I find the need to adapt this Studienförderungsgesetz to the circumstances. It is clear and clear in the report on the social situation of students why there are not as many students as before. On the one hand, it is very much because of the social situation. For example, students who come from outside and therefore have to take up an apartment at the place of study may also have to come from a lower social layer to be able to afford their studies. In addition, there are a lot of students who have started studying in addition to their profession. To all of this, our economy also demands the graduates' professional experience. Therefore, it is absolutely necessary and conclusive that our students enter the professional life. And if we still consider all that was decided in this House at the beginning of this plenary week, we know that it is also necessary to take care of insurance years in good time. At our universities, however, there is – and this seems to me to be a very essential approach – not always all the framework conditions that ensure that a full-time student can continue to benefit from his study aid. I would like to illustrate this, Mrs Federal Minister, on the basis of an example that has been given today at the University of Vienna. A student has long prepared for the exam to be held today. The appointment was made with the professor, but she had forgotten until the 29th century. In June, in accordance with the bureaucracy, register in the firm. The result: no test possible. If these conditions are not changed at our universities, students will also fall from the reference of a scholarship due to circumstances that they have not themselves caused. All in all, it was tried by law to distribute the existing resources fairly fairly. And I consider it quite positive that after the Backalaureat study, an associated Master's or Doctor's degree also means the right for further recognition of study funding. However, I find it somewhat worrying that we also promote the study of students at private universities, because if someone can afford these very high tuition fees, then it must be assumed that he has this standard of living, or if he pays for this direction of study, other students who are really in need of scholarships. It should be seen as positive that, despite the change of studies, study funding will continue to be paid. However, I am concerned about the fact that we tend to be in private administration in the course of graduation scholarships, which is a risk that there is no legal claim on it. Overall, I consider this innovation to be a cost-neutral undertaking, which unfortunately will not all students be very excited about.

However, I do not want to continue to deal with these incidents, but I believe that for us social-democratic education politicians this incident should be the cause and reason to think about how to prevent or make such a thing better. That is why I would like to try to make a constructive proposal with very few comments, and I would like to invite you to discuss it. Ladies and gentlemen. Political education is currently enshrined in the Austrian curriculum as a teaching principle. This means that all teachers should take up appropriate teaching situations in order to take up different elements of political education in teaching time and again. However, this kind of political education is obviously toothless or too little offensive and too little effective as a teaching principle. Therefore, I would like to make two points in the room: Variant one: Let us try to strengthen the teaching principle of political education by giving the teaching principle – and this is not usual so far – a clear curriculum task, i.e. setting target formulations, and adding a binding time frame to the teaching principle of political education, for example, as we have done in the field of vocational orientation. Let us also try to take up and revive a working school partnership in school – we have submitted a request that has not yet been finally treated – in order to actually create a permanent and interesting political training and experience for young people in school. Variant two: I know that from various sides there is also the desire to introduce the field of political education at school. In other words, experts would teach political education for an hour, I suppose, in the week. I therefore do not think this is so happy, because I fear that all other colleagues who work in a school, in a class, will always have the excuses: We have an expert here, and we don't want to talk about that. He's in charge. This is the one danger I see. The second danger I see is that the other teachers do not feel responsible. Finally, I see the danger that political education – and it certainly should not be – will become a cognitive learning subject, where knowledge will then be checked. We social-democratic education politicians believe that democracy is living, democracy is involved, responsibility is taken for others, for the weaker in school, and finally in society is the right approach. And as a third variant, I could imagine that we also see this as a challenge and strive to carry out an information offensive, an information campaign on recent history also at Austrian schools, with the help of the daily newspapers, television and other media. Thank you.

At the same time, the University Accreditation Act was adopted which regulates the recognition of private universities. These changes also require a change to the Studienförderungsgesetz, which we will agree today, as I hope, to do. Students who have opted for a Backalaureat study will also have a right to study aid if they join a Master's degree in the future. I think this is especially necessary because today's young people very often decide to study for a longer period of time and to complete a study different from the original Bakkalaureat study, especially because only now are the starting points for the baccalaureat studies which were set up at the beginning. The condition is that the Master's degree was completed quickly, and the Bakkalaureat study did not take too long. There will also be the possibility of receiving study aid as a student at a private university based in Austria. As a Tyrolean, I am particularly pleased that students who study in South Tyrol will also be able to receive study aid. Tyrol and South Tyrol have very closely coordinated their range of higher education courses and the university programme, which is under construction in South Tyrol. Due to the spatial proximity and the German language on both sides of the border, the different vocational universities and courses complement each other, so that it is now much easier for Austrians to study abroad within the European region. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and on representatives of the Free Trade Unions.]] This means that our education offer can be expanded at the same time. By the way, the South Tyrol university education offer – unlike it is currently at the Innsbruck universities – already has some innovations that we will implement in the near future in Austria. There is already the possibility to receive multilingual teaching at the state universities within a single state university course, not only in German and Italian, but also in English and French. I like the fact that the Austrians can now make greater use of this offer. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and on representatives of the Free Trade Unions.]] Many students have already accepted part-time jobs, on the one hand, in order to become fully qualified for work later on, on the other hand because they depend on making money in addition to their studies. In future, they will also have the opportunity to complete their studies quickly by granting study aid for the last year of their studies if they give up their professional activity in order to achieve a rapid degree. I am therefore pleased that this will further reduce the significantly reduced study termination rates in Austria over the last few years. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and on representatives of the Free Trade Unions.]] This amendment to the Studienförderungsgesetz makes it easier for students to benefit from the appropriate study offer and to shorten their study time. The extension and flexibility of the right to study grants is in the sense of all students, but especially in the interests of the socially weak and in the interests of those who are still moving a little away from the mainstream and who have opted for a study of the Bakkalaureat or at a private university or at a university in South Tyrol. This will take a further step towards our goal of reducing the number of drop-outs, ensuring social justice and dealing with the lifetime of young people. Dear ladies and gentlemen! I am pleased that all parties will agree to this law. Thank you.

(b) the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women. This withdrawal was possible because women were given the opportunity to serve in the military. We welcome this, but think that of the three questions for those who are relatively least important. The second question, which concerns it and which is quite important, is the question that – at last, one must say – the session duration of those committees which carry out the review of the conduct of the Member States of the Convention is extended. That committee, whose task it is to observe and, if necessary, act as a stammer in the meat of the Member States of the Convention, in order to enforce it or create additional arguments and additional pressure, that discrimination against women will finally be eliminated, that committee, which has been able to meet for only two weeks a year, can now meet in more detailed sessions of its work, which was also necessary in view of the fact that 165 states have ratified this Convention. But the most important of the three measures to be decided here today is the Optional Protocol. The optional protocol – with details, Mr Prammer will still deal with each other – creates the possibility of an individual complaint. Here, women themselves can at last claim their concern, their complaint against the fact that they are still being discriminated against in their Member States, although they have ratified the Convention. I think this is an essential step in which, by the way, Austrian women have been very much involved in the preparation process. They must not only pay high recognition, but also thank them. [[Congratulations with the SPÖ.]] I would, however, like to point out, essentially, only one point, and that is the point I am looking forward to today. With the withdrawal of Austrian reservations under these two conventions or conventions, we also contribute to the fact that women in other countries have better arguments and more pressure to force their respective countries to overcome measures that are still permitted there because of existing reservations, finally enforce, so to speak, that their countries too give up their reservations. My Dear Ones Ladies and gentlemen! From the recent deliberations of the Convention on the creation of a European Charter of Fundamental Rights, I am only too aware of the importance of having as many countries as possible a common legal system as possible when it comes to protection against discrimination, in particular against women. It is one of the key arguments that can help in extending and safeguarding fundamental rights. If it succeeds in withdrawing as many states as possible, it is a help to all those who are still in a more difficult situation today. That is why we gladly agree with today's ratifications, because we are convinced that this will help others, not just Austrian women.

These objections refer to the fact that Dr Kostelka's amendments were not wrongly voted on. I would point out, however, that the President-in-Office of the Council, after a break in the sitting, has made two votes, so that I consider these two points to be rehabilitated. A third and a fourth point is added to the objections and points out that another request made by Mr Kostelka on Art. 2 Z 8 of the motion for a resolution was not put to the vote. This means that requested amendments relating to the date of the in-force step of the template have not been voted on. The so-called anti-corruption on Art. However, 2 Z 8 as amended by the Committee's report has taken place. I will take account of the objections to the Official Protocol insofar as it complements the official protocol, that of the amendment tabled by Mr Kostelka, who in his own way. 2 Z 8 was referred to, mistakenly not voted on, on the corresponding text as amended by the Committee's report, and on the other hand, that a non-committee report. ParlaMint-AT_2000-07-07-021-XXI-NRSITZ-00034_d7e17024	Ladies and gentlemen! We arrived at the end of the last scheduled session before the summer break – certainly after hard and intensive work, but at a reasonable time. As we all know, this National Council is 3rd. ==References====External links== He held seven meetings in the course of the government negotiations by the beginning of 2000, from the beginning of February to today he held another 27 meetings and passed 84 laws during this period. It is not my intention now to highlight individual legislative decisions in particular, but I am allowed – because I can only do it now – to express my joy at the fact that we have unanimously adopted the Reconciliation Fund Act today. This law and, of course, all other laws have come about as a product of cooperation between many institutions and many people. I would therefore like to say a very warm word of thanks to our colleagues here today, both from the Parliamentary Directorate and from the political groups, on this occasion, at this time and before this full plenary. [[General condolences.]] I would also like to thank all those who work outside the house, but who work closely with the legislative bodies of the Republic of Austria, including the representatives of broadcasting, television and all the other media. A warm thank you too! [[General condolences.]] Personally, I can also join the members of the Presidential Conference for those – how should I say? – sometimes not simple, but in almost all cases ultimately on results, which, as I believe, I thank for good results, leading cooperation. That we will end this meeting of the National Council will sometimes trigger misunderstandings, and I therefore explicitly note that this does not mean that a two-month, completely unmistakable break is now taking place, but – on the contrary! – a large number of work and commitments are continued. I refer to the next week with a meeting of the main committee, a presidential conference, a meeting with foreign guests and the Constitutional Committee which has just been declared permanent. There's more to this. Nevertheless – or precisely that – I wish you, ladies and gentlemen, and also your family a relaxing break, however long it may be, good hours, time to consider everything, and then a return to work with fresh forces. I can also wish all our fellow citizens a nice and pleasant summer on this occasion. In this sense, I conclude the meeting with warm greetings to all of you.
